=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
This problem is a sub-problem of Problem F (Operate K), with K=1.
You can solve this problem by submitting a correct solution for Problem F to this problem.
Determine whether it is possible to perform the following operation on string S between 0 and K times, inclusive, to make it identical to string T.

- Choose one of the following three operations and execute it.
- Insert any one character at any position in S (possibly the beginning or end).
- Delete one character from S.
- Choose one character in S and replace it with another character.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
K
S
T

Output

If S can be made identical to T with at most K operations, print Yes; otherwise, print No.

Constraints


- Each of S and T is a string of length between 1 and 500000, inclusive, consisting of lowercase English letters.
- \color{red}{K=1}

Sample Input 1

1
abc
agc

Sample Output 1

Yes

Replacing the second character b of abc with g converts abc to agc in one operation.

Sample Input 2

1
abc
awtf

Sample Output 2

No

abc cannot be converted to awtf in one operation.

Sample Input 3

1
abc
ac

Sample Output 3

Yes

Deleting the second character b of abc converts abc to ac in one operation.

Sample Input 4

1
back
black

Sample Output 4

Yes

Inserting l between the first and second characters of back converts back to black in one operation.

Sample Input 5

1
same
same

Sample Output 5

Yes

It is also possible that S = T from the beginning.

Sample Input 6

1
leap
read

Sample Output 6

No

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's maybe formulate code:

import sys

def can_one_delete(longer, shorter):
    i = j = 0
    skipped = False
    ln = len(longer)
    ls = len(shorter)
    while i < ln and j < ls:
        if longer[i] == shorter[j]:
            i += 1
            j += 1
        else:
            if skipped:
                return False
            skipped = True
            i += 1
    return True

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    # K = int(data[0])
    S = data[1]
    T = data[2]
    n = len(S)
    m = len(T)
    if n == m:
        diff = 0
        for a,b in zip(S,T):
            if a != b:
                diff += 1
                if diff > 1:
                    print("No")
                    return
        print("Yes")
    elif n == m + 1:
        print("Yes" if can_one_delete(S, T) else "No")
    elif m == n + 1:
        print("Yes" if can_one_delete(T, S) else "No")
    else:
        print("No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

import sys

def ok(a,b):
    if len(a) < len(b):
        a,b = b,a
    if len(a) - len(b) > 1:
        return False
    if len(a) == len(b):
        return sum(x != y for x,y in zip(a,b)) <= 1
    i=j=0; skipped=False
    while i < len(a) and j < len(b):
        if a[i] == b[j]:
            i += 1; j += 1
        else:
            if skipped:
                return False
            skipped = True
            i += 1
    return True

Let's decide final code structure:
import sys

def can_delete_one(longer, shorter):
    i = j = 0
    skipped = False
    n = len(longer)
    m = len(shorter)
    while i < n and j < m:
        if longer[i] == shorter[j]:
            i += 1
            j += 1
        else:
            if skipped:
                return False
            skipped = True
            i += 1
    return True

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    # K = int(data[0])
    S = data[1]
    T = data[2]
    if S == T:
        print("Yes")
        return
    n = len(S)
    m = len(T)
    if n == m:
        diff = 0
        for a, b in zip(S, T):
            if a != b:
                diff += 1
                if diff > 1:
                    print("No")
                    return
        print("Yes")
    elif n == m + 1:
        print("Yes" if can_delete_one(S, T) else "No")
    elif m == n + 1:
        print("Yes" if can_delete_one(T, S) else "No")
    else:
        print("No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's decide final code with K check:
import sys

def can_delete_one(longer, shorter):
    i = j = 0
    skipped = False
    while i < len(longer) and j < len(shorter):
        if longer[i] == shorter[j]:
            i += 1
            j += 1
        else:
            if skipped:
                return False
            skipped = True
            i += 1
    return True

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    K = int(data[0])
    S = data[1]
    T = data[2]

    if K == 0:
        print("Yes" if S == T else "No")
        return

    if S == T:
        print("Yes")
        return

    n, m = len(S), len(T)

    if n == m:
        diff = 0
        for a, b in zip(S, T):
            if a != b:
                diff += 1
                if diff > 1:
                    print("No")
                    return
        print("Yes")
    elif n == m + 1:
        print("Yes" if can_delete_one(S, T) else "No")
    elif m == n + 1:
        print("Yes" if can_delete_one(T, S) else "No")
    else:
        print("No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Is there any issue if K==0 and S==T? We return Yes. If K==0 and S!=T but n==m diff=1, return No. Good. If K==0 and S!=T but len diff1, return No. Good.

def can_delete_one(longer, shorter):
    i = j = 0
    skipped = False
    n = len(longer)
    m = len(shorter)
    while i < n and j < m:
        if longer[i] == shorter[j]:
            i += 1
            j += 1
        else:
            if skipped:
                return False
            skipped = True
            i += 1
    return True

Let's maybe think about if there is any issue with `can_delete_one` returning True when `skipped` is True but there are still characters in longer? Let's check. Suppose longer="abc", shorter="ac": after skip b, i=2,j=1, compare c vs c, i=3,j=2, loop false. No remaining. Suppose longer="abxc", shorter="abc": after skip x, i=3,j=2, compare c vs c, i=4,j=3. No remaining. Suppose longer="xabc", shorter="abc": after skip x, i=1,j=0, then match a,b,c, i=4,j=3. No remaining. Since longer length = shorter+1, if exactly one skip and all shorter matched, i should be len(longer). Let's verify: Each match increments both, each skip increments i only. If skipped once and j=m, then i = j + skips = m + 1 = n. So no remaining. If skipped false and j=m, i=m = n-1, so one remaining longer char (the last) but loop exits because j==m. Return True. Good.
What if skipped true and j==m but i < n? Could that happen? i = j + skips = m + 1 = n. So no. Good.
What if skipped true and i==n but j < m? Then skips = i - j = n - j > n - m = 1? Since j < m, skips > 1. But our algorithm would have returned False on second mismatch, not exit with i==n and j<m unless no second mismatch? Wait if no second mismatch, every remaining longer char matched a shorter char, so j should reach m before i reaches n? Let's compute: n=m+1. If skipped once and no second mismatch, matches = m, skips=1, i=m+1=n, j=m. So j==m. If j<m, then matches < m, skips >1, which would require second mismatch, but algorithm returns False at second mismatch. So cannot exit with i==n and j<m. Good.

We can enumerate small strings. Let's write a mental brute for A length 3, B length 2. All possible A,B over {a,b}. If B is subsequence of A, algorithm should true. Let's test all B:
B="aa". A="aaa": algorithm match a,a, loop exits true. A="aab": match a,a, loop exits true (delete b). A="aba": match a, mismatch b vs a skip b, match a true. A="baa": mismatch b vs a skip b, match a,a true. All true if B subsequence? A="abb" B="aa" no; algorithm: match a, mismatch b vs a skip b, then b vs a mismatch skipped false -> false. Good.
B="ab". A="aab": match a, mismatch a vs b skip a, match b true. A="aba": match a, mismatch b vs b? Wait A="aba", B="ab": i=0 a match, i=1 b match, loop exits true. A="baa": mismatch b vs a skip b, match a, then a vs b mismatch skipped false -> false. Is "ab" subsequence of "baa"? No. Good.
B="ba". A="baa": match b,a true. A="bba": match b, mismatch b vs a skip b, match a true. A="aba": match a, mismatch b vs b? Actually B="ba": i=0 a vs b mismatch skip a, i=1 b match, i=2 a match true. Good.
